->[Decorative border with floral and scroll motifs]<-

## TO THE READER.

THE Treatise on the **Way of Perfection** composed by **Saint Teresa** <note>Saint Teresa of Avila (1515–1582) was a prominent Spanish mystic, Roman Catholic saint, and author of the Counter-Reformation.</note> appeared so useful and so edifying to several pious individuals, that they desired it to be translated into our language, so that, being printed separately, it might be more easily held in everyone's hands. For although the doctrine of this great Saint is truly celestial, and all her writings deserve to be equally revered: nevertheless, there are found in the others, in some places, things so extraordinarily elevated, that they seem to be less suited to the intelligence and the ordinary piety of the Faithful.

But what is unique in this Book of the **Way of Perfection**, is that it seems that the...

<margin>ã ij</margin> <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the book pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a translation of Saint Teresa of Avila's "The Way of Perfection," explaining that it was published as a standalone volume to make her spiritual teachings more accessible to everyday readers. The author suggests that while Teresa's other works are divinely inspired, they are often too complex for the average person, whereas this text is more practical.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, Way of Perfection, translation, Catholic mysticism, 17th-century literature, piety, devotional books</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface or "To the Reader" section, introducing a spiritual treatise likely written by Saint Teresa of Avila. It establishes the book's dual purpose for both monastics and laypeople.</meta>

->TO THE READER.<-

The Saint <note>This likely refers to Saint Teresa of Avila, whose "Way of Perfection" contains a famous commentary on the Lord's Prayer.</note> has applied herself to humble herself as much as she could in order to make herself more understandable. This is why it can be said of this Work that it is very useful, whether for those in Religious orders and those living in seclusion, or for those who find themselves engaged in the dealings of the world. For the most spiritual and perfect souls will find here excellent rules to advance ever further in the highest virtue; and those who, living in the secular world <note>original: "dans le siecle." In this period, "the century" referred to the secular, material world outside the walls of a monastery.</note>, desire to serve God, will learn here with what respect they must address Him in their prayers. They will also find an admirable explanation of the Our Father <note>original: "Pater." The Latin title for the Lord's Prayer.</note>, which clearly demonstrates, through the excellent instructions it contains, the truth of what an ancient Church Author <note>Probably referring to Tertullian or Saint Augustine, both of whom described the Lord's Prayer as a "summary of the Gospel."</note> once said: that this Prayer of the Lord is the summary of the entire Gospel.

<summary>This preface introduces a spiritual work, emphasizing that its humble and clear style makes it accessible to both cloistered monastics and laypeople living in society. It highlights a specific commentary on the Lord's Prayer as a comprehensive guide to the Christian Gospel.</summary>

<keywords>preface, Saint Teresa of Avila, Lord's Prayer, Pater Noster, monastic life, secular life, spiritual growth, Gospel summary</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface or "To the Reader" for a reprinted devotional work. It establishes the book's popularity (being sold out) and the publisher's intent to maintain the integrity of the original translation. It likely precedes a collection of spiritual reflections by a female saint.</meta>

->### TO THE READER<-

Since it has been observed that the edi-
tion of this book was entirely sold out,
it was judged appropriate to reprint it 
exactly as it was given by its illustrious 
& pious Translator, so that the Pub-
lic might not be deprived of such an excel-
lent guide to the spiritual life.

   It would be unnecessary to recommend 
this Treatise <term>Treatise: A formal, systematic written discourse on a specific subject, in this case, spiritual practice.</term> any further. All those 
who possess any sense of piety 
will not be able to read it without valuing it. 
And they will be truly happy, if—in 
imitation of this admirable Saint <note>This likely refers to a female mystic such as Saint Teresa of Avila, whose writings were often described as coming from the heart rather than scholarly study.</note> 
who drew these thoughts more from 
her heart than from her mind—they 
use them more to nourish 
their piety than to enlighten 
their soul; and if they take 
even more care to practice them than 
to read them.

<summary>This preface explains that the book has been reprinted due to high demand and encourages readers to focus on the practical application of the Saint's "heartfelt" spiritual insights rather than mere intellectual study.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, Spiritual Life, Piety, Devotional Literature, Saint, Translation</keywords>

<meta>This page is the beginning of the "Approbation" (formal approval) for a 17th-century French translation of the works of Saint Teresa of Avila.</meta>

[Decorative border: a row of stylized floral ornaments]

# APPROBATION
## *of the Doctors.*

The favors <note>In this 17th-century religious context, "favors" refers to divine graces or mystical experiences granted by God.</note> that Saint Teresa <note>Saint Teresa of Avila (1515–1582), the Spanish mystic, writer, and reformer of the Carmelite Order.</note> received from Heaven are so extraordinary and so elevated that one cannot easily understand them without having had the experience and the feeling of them in the heart; and whatever idea one might conceive of them, it would be difficult to find words in common language to express them well. 

To recognize their truth, it was necessary that they be examined by Saints who shared the same graces and who had the gift of <term>discerning spirits</term> <note>A theological term for the ability to judge whether a spiritual manifestation or vision is truly from God, or from a human or demonic source.</note>; and it was necessary that this Saint take up the pen, and that she herself write her own history to make them known to the whole Church. She could have no interpreter in our language <note>Refers to the French language.</note> who could better sustain, in a translation, the grandeur of her thoughts and the strength of her words than Monsieur d’Andilly <note>Robert Arnauld d’Andilly (1589–1674), a celebrated French translator and scholar associated with the Jansenist movement at Port-Royal.</note>, who

<summary>This page contains a formal endorsement of Robert Arnauld d'Andilly's French translation of Saint Teresa of Avila's life, asserting that her mystical experiences are so profound they can only be truly expressed by her own hand and a translator of d'Andilly's caliber.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, Robert Arnauld d'Andilly, Approbation, mysticism, discernment of spirits, French translation, 17th-century spirituality</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as a formal endorsement or "Approbation" for the work, a common feature in 17th-century religious texts where established authorities vouched for the orthodoxy and quality of a book before its publication.</meta>

...has distinguished his merit by so many excellent works. We judge that this one corresponds perfectly to the dignity of its subject, and that it is very capable of edifying the faith and the piety of the most Christian and the most spiritual persons. At Paris, this 25th of May 1670.

->A. DEBREDA, Priest <note>The original term is *Curé*, the head priest of a parish.</note> of St. André.<-

->T. FORTIN, Principal <note>The *Proviseur* was the administrative head of a college.</note> of the College of Harcourt.<-

->GRENET, Priest of St. Benoit.<-

->P. MARLIN, Priest of St. Eustache.<-

->N. GOBILLON, Doctor of the Sorbonne <note>The prestigious theological faculty of the University of Paris.</note>, Priest of St. Laurent.<-

<meta>This page follows the title page and provides the legal documentation for the book's publication, establishing the chain of ownership for the printing rights.</meta>

[Horizontal line]

*Extract of the King’s Privilege.* <note>A "Privilege" was a royal decree granting a printer the exclusive right to publish a specific work, serving as an early form of copyright law to prevent unauthorized "counterfeiting" or reprinting.</note>

**B** Y grace & Privilege of His Ma-
jesty granted at the King’s Council of State,
held at Versailles on August 3,
1675. permission is granted to PIERRE LE PE-
TIT, his Ordinary Printer, to
print or reprint the works of
Saint Teresa, in the translation of
Mr. Arnauld d’Andilly <note>Robert Arnauld d'Andilly (1589–1674) was a major figure in the Jansenist movement and a celebrated translator of spiritual and historical texts.</note>, for the
period & duration of fifty years,
with prohibitions against all others counter-
feiting the said works, under the penalties set
forth in the Original of the said Privilege.

The aforementioned Privilege was assigned &
transferred to Mr. Denys Thierry,
Printer & Bookseller in Paris, as
it appears by the official report of the
sale of the assets <note>Original: "Fond" — the business holdings, including the legal rights to specific titles.</note> of the said Mr. le Petit by
Ganneau, Sergeant-at-Mace <note>Original: "Sergent à Verge" — an officer of the court who carried a rod or "verge" as a symbol of royal authority.</note> at the Châtelet
of Paris <note>The Châtelet was the main courthouse and headquarters of the police and legal administration in Paris under the Old Regime.</note>, on April 21, 1687.

And Mr. Roulland has acquired the afore-
mentioned Privilege from Mr. Thierry,
to enjoy it in his stead.

*FORE-* <note>This is a "catchword," a printing convention where the first word or syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to assist the binder in arranging the pages correctly.</note>

<summary>This page records the royal "Privilege" or copyright for the works of Saint Teresa in French translation, documenting how the printing rights were legally transferred between Parisian booksellers from 1675 to 1687.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Avant-Propos" or Foreword of the work, likely "The Way of Perfection" by St. Teresa of Avila.</meta>

### FOREWORD
->by the Saint<-

The Sisters of this Monastery of Saint Joseph of Avila, knowing that Father *Presentado* <note>original: "Pere Preſenté"; a formal academic title within the Dominican Order for a friar who has completed his theological requirements to become a Master of Sacred Theology.</note> Dominic Bañez <note>original: "Dominique Bagnez"; a prominent Spanish Dominican theologian and the primary confessor of St. Teresa during this period.</note>, a religious of the Order of the glorious Saint Dominic, who is currently my confessor, has permitted me to write about prayer <note>original: "l'Oraiſon"; specifically referring to "mental prayer" or contemplative prayer, a central theme in Teresa's spirituality.</note>, they believed that I could do so usefully, because I have discussed this subject with several very spiritual and very holy people; and they have urged me so much to tell them something about it, that I have resolved to obey them, because the great love they bear for me will make them better receive what will come from me, however imperfect and poorly written it may be, than books whose style is excellent, and which were written by men very learned in this matter. I put my confidence in their prayers, which may perhaps obtain from God that, by giving me the means to [tell] them...

->to<- <note>This "é" at the bottom of the page is a catchword, representing the first syllable of the word on the following page—likely "écrire" (to write) or "édifier" (to edify).</note>

<summary>In this foreword, Saint Teresa of Avila explains that she is writing this treatise on prayer at the request of her fellow nuns, trusting that their affection for her will make her simple words more effective than scholarly texts.</summary>
<keywords>St. Teresa of Avila, St. Joseph of Avila, Dominic Bañez, mental prayer, Dominican Order, spiritual direction, monastic life</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from a previous page where the author was discussing the command to write her observations.</meta>

### ->Foreword.<-

[to] give, I will say something useful regarding the way of life practiced in this house <note>Refers to the Convent of Saint Joseph in Avila, the first monastery of the Discalced Carmelite reform.</note>. If I happen to speak poorly, Father Bañez <note>original: "le Pere Bagnez"; Domingo Bañez (1528–1604) was a prominent Dominican theologian and the author's confessor.</note>, who will be the first to see it, will correct it or burn it. Thus, I will lose nothing for having obeyed these servants of God; and they will see what I am capable of on my own when His grace does not assist me.

My intention is to teach remedies for slight temptations stirred up by the devil, which religious <term>religious: individuals who have taken formal vows in a monastic order, such as monks or nuns</term> persons take no account of because they do not believe them to be significant; and to also treat other points as our Lord gives me understanding and as I am able to remember them. For, not knowing what I have to say, I could not say it in order; and I believe it is best not to keep any order at all, since it is already such a great reversal of the natural order that I should even undertake to write on such a subject.

I implore God’s assistance so that I may conform myself entirely to His holy will. This is what [my thoughts] tend toward...

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the foreword to Saint Teresa of Avila’s "The Way of Perfection," where she explains her purpose: writing out of obedience to her sisters and under the supervision of her confessor, Father Bañez.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, Father Domingo Bañez, monastic life, spiritual guidance, temptations, obedience, Carmelite reform</keywords>

...to love one another exceedingly. Avoid with great care all **singularities** <note>original: "singularitez"; in a monastic context, this refers to idiosyncratic behaviors or habits that set an individual apart from the communal life</note> and **partialities** <note>original: "partialitez"; favoritism or exclusive "particular friendships" which were often discouraged in religious orders to maintain community unity</note>. **CHAP. VI.** On the spiritual love that one ought to have for God, and for those who can contribute to our salvation. p. 57

**CHAP. VII.** On the admirable qualities of the spiritual love that holy persons have for the souls to whom God binds them. What happiness it is to share in their friendship. Of the compassion that even the most perfect souls must have for the weaknesses of others. Various pieces of advice concerning the manner in which **Nuns** <note>original: "Religieuses"; specifically women living under religious vows in a community</note> should conduct themselves. And with what promptness and severity one must repress desires for honor and preference. p. 66

*By the Saint.*

all my desires, even though my actions do not match them. But at least I do not lack the affection and ardor to help with all my power my dear Sisters to advance more and more in the service of God. This love that I have for them, joined with my age and my experience of what happens in some Religious Houses <note>convents or monasteries</note>, will perhaps allow me to succeed better in small matters than the Scholars <note>original: "Sçavans"; referring here to male theologians and university-educated confessors</note>. Because they have other more important occupations and are strong people, they do not take much account of those imperfections which appear to be nothing in themselves; they do not consider that since women are weak, everything is capable of harming them. Furthermore, the tricks <note>original: "artifices"</note> that the devil uses against Nuns who are so strictly enclosed <note>referring to "enclosure" or "cloister," where religious women live separated from the outside world</note> are many in number, because he knows he needs new weapons to fight them. And as I have defended myself so poorly against them—being as flawed as I am—I wish that my Sisters might profit from my mistakes.

*Foreword by the Saint.*

I shall say nothing that I have not recognized through experience, either in myself or in others. And although I was commanded just a few days ago to write an account of my life, where I also included some advice regarding Prayer <term>Oraison: In this context, it refers to "mental prayer," a form of contemplative, internal conversation with God rather than the recitation of set texts</term>; nevertheless, because my Confessor <note>St. Teresa wrote her major works under the "holy obedience" of her spiritual directors, who often reviewed and censored her manuscripts</note> might not want you to see it right now, I will repeat some of it here and will add other things that seem necessary to me. May our Lord be pleased to assist me, as I have already prayed to Him, and cause everything I write to succeed for His greater glory.

2 ->THE WAY<-

...[re]quired so many outward austerities <note>Strict physical disciplines or harsh living conditions often practiced by religious orders.</note>, nor that it should be without a steady income. On the contrary, I desired that, if it were possible, nothing should be lacking of all the necessary things, acting in this like a weak and imperfect person, although I was moved more by a good intention than by the desire for an easier and more relaxed life.

At this same time, having learned of the troubles in France <note>A reference to the French Wars of Religion (1562–1598), a period of intense civil conflict between Catholics and Huguenots (Protestants).</note>, the devastation caused there by the heretics, and how much this unfortunate sect <note>original: "malheureuſe ſecte"; Saint Teresa is referring to the Protestant Reformers from her perspective as a Catholic reformer.</note> was strengthening itself day by day; I was so deeply moved by it that—as if I could have done something, or were someone of importance myself—I wept in the presence of God and prayed to Him to remedy such a great evil. It seemed to me that I would have given a thousand lives to save just one of that great number of souls who were being lost in that Kingdom. 

But seeing that I was only a woman, and moreover so flawed and incapable of rendering to my God the service I desired, I believed—as I still believe—that since He has so many enemies and so few f[riends]... <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("amis" or friends), continuing onto the next page.</note>

...such? For who can doubt that these persons are already under the power of the demon? They are worthy of this punishment, since their deeds have deserved it; and it is quite reasonable that their delights and their false pleasures should have eternal fire for their reward. I confess, however, that I cannot see so many souls being lost without being overwhelmed <note>The original French "outrée" implies being pierced with an intense, agonizing grief or sense of outrage.</note> with sorrow. ->6	THE WAY<-

I laugh, or rather, I am saddened to see what some people come to recommend so insistently to our prayers, even desiring that we ask God for money and income for them; whereas I would like, on the contrary, to pray that He grant them the grace to trample all these things underfoot. I want to believe that their intention is not bad, and one gives in to what they wish; but I hold it for certain that God never grants my prayers on such occasions. All of Christendom is on fire <note>St. Teresa uses the metaphor of "fire" to describe the social and religious upheaval of the 16th-century Protestant Reformation</note>: these wretched heretics <note>original: "heretiques"; in this context, Teresa refers to the rise of Protestantism in Europe, which she viewed as a direct attack on the Catholic Church</note> wish, so to speak, to condemn JESUS CHRIST a second time, since they raise up a thousand false witnesses against Him and work to overthrow His Church. And shall we waste our time on requests which, if God granted them to us, might perhaps only serve to close the gate of Heaven to a soul? No, certainly, my Sisters, this is not the time to negotiate with God for such unimportant matters; and if it were not necessary to have some regard for the weakness of

### CHAPTER II.

*That the Sisters must not trouble themselves with their temporal needs. On the advantages found in poverty. Against grand buildings.*

**D**o not imagine, my Sisters, that by failing to please the people of the world, you will lack what you need to live. <note>Teresa is addressing the fear that without wealthy patrons or "pleasing" society, the convent would fail financially.</note> Never attempt to sustain your House through human schemes and cleverness; otherwise, you will die of hunger, and rightly so. Simply fix your eyes upon your divine Spouse, for it is He who must provide for you.

But even if you were to die of hunger while conducting yourselves in this manner: oh, how blessed would the Sisters <term>Religieuses: Women who have taken religious vows and live in a community.</term> of Saint Joseph be! I implore you in the name of God to engrave these words in your memory: since you have renounced having an income <term>Income: "Revenu" refers to fixed endowments or property that provided a guaranteed livelihood for a convent, which this reformed order rejected in favor of poverty.</term>, renounce also the worry over what concerns your food. If you do not do this, you are lost.

As for us, my Daughters, it would be folly. For would it not be worrying about things that belong to others to think of these incomes? And would your worries inspire in people a desire they do not already have to engage them in giving you charity? Leave this care to Him who rules over

### ON PERFECTION. Ch. II. 5

...the heart, and who is no less the master of riches than of the rich. It is by his order <note>Referring to God’s divine will or providence</note> that we have come here. His words are true, are infallible, and Heaven and earth will pass away rather than they fail to be accomplished <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 24:35</note>.

   Let us only take care not to fail in what we owe him, and do not fear that he will fail in what he has promised us. But even if that <note>Meaning the failure of material support or the arrival of extreme want</note> were to happen, it would undoubtedly be for our advantage; just as the glory of the Saints was increased by martyrdom. Oh, what a happy exchange it would be to die soon for lack of having the means to live, in order to enjoy all the sooner a life and a happiness that will never end.

   Weigh well, I pray you, my **Sisters**, the importance of this advice that I leave you in writing, so that you may remember it after my death: for while I am in the world I will not fail to frequently remind you of it, because I know by experience the advantage there is in practicing it. The less we have,

<summary>In this passage, the author encourages her fellow nuns to trust entirely in God's providence for their material needs, arguing that even death by poverty would be a glorious martyrdom leading to eternal life.</summary>

->12 [space] THE WAY<-

...[pau]per in spirit, as I had made a vow to be, I was for a long time foolish in spirit <note>original: "folle d'esprit." Teresa is likely contrasting the "poverty of spirit" she sought with her own earlier spiritual struggles or lack of focus</note>: and thus the more I was deprived of such a great good, the more I recognized through experience that it is an extreme happiness for a soul to possess it.

   This blessed poverty is so great a good that it encompasses all the goods of the world. Yes, I say it again, it encompasses all the goods of the world, since to despise the world is to be the master of the world. For what would I care to have the favor of the Great and of Princes <note>In the 16th century, the "Great" referred to the high nobility and those with significant political power</note> if I desired neither to have their goods, nor to enjoy their delights, and if I would be very grieved to do anything to please them which could displease God in the slightest thing? How could I also desire their vain honors, knowing that the greatest honor of a poor person consists in being truly poor? I hold that honors and riches almost always go hand in hand: He who loves honor cannot hate riches; and he who despises riches cares little for honor.

<summary>In this passage, the author reflects on the spiritual liberation found in the vow of poverty, arguing that renouncing worldly wealth and status grants a person true mastery over the world.</summary>
<keywords>poverty of spirit, religious vows, humility, renunciation, worldly honors, Saint Teresa of Avila, spiritual wisdom</keywords>

->ON PERFECTION. Ch. II. 13<-

Understand this well, I pray you.

To me, it seems that honor is always followed by some interest in wealth. <note>The original French "interest de bien" implies material goods or financial profit; Teresa is arguing that worldly "honor" is usually just a byproduct of being rich.</note> For it happens very rarely that a poor person is honored in the world, even though their virtue makes them worthy of it; on the contrary, they are held in very little account. 

But as for <term>true poverty</term> <note>This refers to the "voluntary poverty" practiced by the Discalced Carmelites, who gave up all personal property to rely entirely on God.</note>, it is accompanied by a certain honor, which ensures it is a burden to no one. By this poverty, I mean that which is suffered solely for the love of God, and which seeks to please only Him. One never lacks many friends when one has need of no one; I know this by experience. 

But since so many excellent things have already been written about this virtue—more than I could hope to express with my own words, since I lack sufficient light <note>A common spiritual metaphor for intellectual or divine understanding.</note> to fully comprehend it, and besides, I would fear diminishing its value by attempting to praise it—I will content myself with what I have said of my own experience. And I confess that until now, I have found myself in such a way

[from the] wealth of the poor? But may God not permit, if it please Him, that we should possess anything but what is vile and poor. As for those who construct grand buildings, they know their own reasons; and they may have holy intentions that I am not aware of: but the smallest little corner can suffice for thirteen poor Nuns <note>Teresa originally limited her reformed convents to thirteen sisters to ensure a small, intimate community that could live in true poverty.</note>.

   However, if because of the strict enclosure <term>enclosure: the sacred area of a convent where the sisters live in seclusion from the outside world</term> there is a need for some grounds to build hermitages <note>Small, simple huts or outdoor shrines used for private meditation.</note> for praying in solitude, as this can undoubtedly aid in prayer and devotion, I consent to it gladly. But as for grand buildings and having anything curious <note>original: "curieux"—in this context, it refers to things that are ornate, elaborate, or unnecessarily decorative.</note>, may God keep us from it by His grace. Keep continually before your eyes that all the buildings of the world will fall on the Day of Judgment, and that we do not know if that day is near. Now, what likelihood would there be that the house of thirteen poor girls could not fall without making a great noise?

# ON PERFECTION. Ch. II. 17

You are therefore obligated to pray much for those who give you the means to live; for even though the alms come to you from God, He wishes for you to be grateful to those through whom He gives them to you: and you must never fail in this duty.

I do not know what I had begun to say, because I have made a long digression. But I believe our Lord has permitted it, since I had never intended to write what I have just told you. I pray His divine Majesty to always hold us by the hand, so that we may never abandon Him.

### ON PERFECTION. Ch. III. 29

...I implore your clemency? But Lord, since you are a God of mercy, have pity on this poor sinner, this <term>worm of the earth<note>A traditional expression of extreme humility and insignificance before the Divine, frequently used by Saint Teresa of Avila to contrast human frailty with God's majesty.</note></term>; and forgive my boldness. Do not consider my sins: consider rather my desires and the tears I shed while offering you this prayer. I conjure you by your own self. Have pity on so many souls who are being lost: Succor, Lord, your Church: stop the course of so many evils that afflict <term>Christendom<note>In this 16th-century context, this refers to the collective body of Christian nations, then deeply divided by the wars of religion and the Reformation.</note></term>; and make your light shine among these shadows.

I ask you, my Sisters, for the love of JESUS CHRIST and as a matter to which you are duty-bound, to pray to His divine Majesty for this poor and far too bold sinner who speaks to you, so that it may please Him to give me the humility that is necessary for me. As for the King and the <term>Prelates<note>High-ranking officials of the Church, such as bishops or archbishops.</note></term> of the Church, and particularly our Bishop, I do not recommend them to you at all, because I see you so diligent in praying for them that I do not believe there is any need for it.

For how must we be in order not to be considered reckless in the judgment of God and men? For there is reason to hope that Our Lord will grant our prayers, provided that we do not neglect anything within our power to observe our <term>Constitutions</term> <note>The specific administrative laws and statutes governing a religious order.</note> and our <term>Rule</term> <note>The fundamental spiritual guidelines of the order; for the author, this refers to the Carmelite Rule.</note> exactly. This fault occurs even more frequently, in my opinion, among women than among men, and brings a visible harm to the whole Community, because it follows from this that one does not love all the Sisters equally: that one feels the hurt given to one's friend: that one desires to have something to give her: that one seeks the opportunity to speak to her, most often without having anything to say to her, except that one loves her, and other trivial things <note>original: "impertinentes" — in this 17th-century context, this means "irrelevant" or "frivolous" rather than "disrespectful."</note>, rather than speaking to her of the love one ought to have for God.

For when people love each other only to serve His Divine Majesty, the effects soon make it known; for whereas others love each other to satisfy their passion, these women seek instead, through the affection they bear each other, a remedy to conquer their passions <term>passions: in 17th-century spiritual writing, "passions" refer to unruly emotions or worldly attachments that distract the soul from divine focus.</term>.

	As for this last kind of friendship, I wish that in large monasteries many such friendships could be found. ->OF PERFECTION. Ch. IV. 39<-

...when one is alone; furthermore, it is extremely important to grow accustomed to solitude in order to practice Prayer <term>Prayer (Oraison): In this context, it refers to "mental prayer" or internal contemplation, which Saint Teresa established as the primary work of her reformed convents.</term> effectively. There are no people so coarse <note>The original French uses "brutales," implying a lack of refinement or spiritual sensibility.</note> that—while living and communicating together constantly, having no conversations, interviews, or amusements with those from the outside world (nor being permitted any), and having every reason to believe that God loves these Sisters and that they love Him, since they have left everything for His love—could fail to love one another. 

Moreover, it is the nature of virtue to make itself loved, and I hope, by the grace of God, that virtue will never abandon this Monastery.

## 40 THE WAY

->THE WAY<-

I do not think, then, that there is much need to urge you to love one another in the manner I have just described. But I want to show you what this praiseworthy love is that I desire to see among us, and by what signs we can know that we have acquired this virtue—which must be of great importance, since Our Lord recommended it so expressly to His Apostles. This is what I will now discuss with you for a little while, according to my limited capacity. <note>The author frequently employs "humility formulas," a common 17th-century literary device where the writer (especially a woman) downplays her own intellect to remain humble before the reader and the Church.</note> If you find it better explained in other books, do not dwell on what I write about it. For perhaps I do not even know what I am saying.

<margin>On affection for Confessors</margin> <note>A "Confessor" was a priest who heard a person's confession of sins and often provided ongoing spiritual guidance or "direction."</note>

There are two kinds of love I will speak of. One is purely spiritual, with nothing appearing in it that might tarnish its purity, because it contains nothing of <term>sensuality</term> <note>In this context, "sensuality" refers to the "lower" part of human nature—the physical senses and emotions—as opposed to the "higher" intellectual or spiritual soul.</note> or the tenderness of our nature. The other is also spiritual, but our sensuality and our weakness are mixed into it. It is, however, a good love, and appears legitimate; such is the love seen between relatives and friends. ### ON PERFECTION. Ch. IV. 41

[I] said something of this latter type, and I now wish to speak of the other, which is purely spiritual and without any mixture of passion. For if any passion were to be found there, all the spirituality that appeared to be in it would vanish and become sensual; whereas if we conduct ourselves in this other love—though it be less perfect—with moderation and with prudence, everything in it will be meritorious <note>meritorious: an act considered worthy of spiritual reward or "merit" in a religious context</note>, and what appeared to be sensuality will be transformed into virtue.

But this sensuality sometimes mingles there so subtly that it is difficult to discern, especially if it happens to be in relation to a Confessor <note>Confessor: a priest who hears confessions and acts as a spiritual director or guide</note>, because people who devote themselves to Prayer <term>Prayer: the original French "Oraiſon" refers to "Mental Prayer," a form of internal, contemplative communication with God</term> become extremely attached to the one who governs their conscience when they recognize in him much virtue and the ability to lead them. 

It is here that the demon besieges them with a great number of scruples <term>scruples: obsessive anxiety or "nit-picking" about whether one has committed a small sin, often leading to spiritual paralysis</term> with the intent of making them anxious and troubled; and especially if he sees that the Confessor leads them toward a greater perfection. For then he pressures them in such a way that he...

->42      THE WAY<-

...makes them resolve to leave their <term>Confessor</term><note>A priest who hears a person's confession and offers spiritual guidance. In this period, the bond between a nun and her confessor was central to her religious life.</note>, and does not leave them at rest even after they have chosen another.

   What these persons can do in this state is not to focus on discerning whether they love or do not love. If they love, let them love. For if we love those from whom we receive benefits that only concern the body, why should we not love those who work unceasingly to procure for us the benefits of the soul? I believe, on the contrary, that it is a sign that one is beginning to make notable progress when one loves their Confessor when he is holy and spiritual, and when one sees that he works to make us advance in virtue; our weakness being such that we could often not undertake great things for the service of God without his help.

   But if the Confessor is not such as I have just described, then it is that there is much danger, and that a very great evil can happen from his seeing that he is cherished, especially—

<summary>This page argues that having a spiritual affection for a holy and virtuous confessor is a sign of religious progress rather than a sin, as such a guide is essential for spiritual growth. However, it warns of significant danger if the confessor lacks holiness.</summary>

### ON PERFECTION. Ch. V. 55

<margin>On the authority of Superiors.</margin>

Among the inconveniences that can occur, as some are always encountered everywhere during this life, it seems to me that the least [evil] is that there should be no Vicar nor Confessor who has the power to enter, to command, and to leave, but only to watch and to take care that the House remains in recollection <note>Recollection: a state of spiritual focus and inner silence, essential to the contemplative life</note>, that all things are done there with propriety, and that one advances interiorly and exteriorly in the practice of virtue; so that if it is found that there is a failing, he informs the Bishop; but that he should not be the Superior. 

This is what is observed now here, not by my opinion alone, but by that of My Lord Don Alvarez de Mendoza <note>original: "Dom Alvarez de Mendoçe" — Don Álvaro de Mendoza, the Bishop of Avila and a major patron of Teresa's Discalced Carmelite reform</note>, now our Bishop and under whose guidance we are, a person of very noble birth, a great servant of God, very devoted to everything concerning the Religious Life <term>Religion: in this context, the term refers specifically to the structured life and rules of a monastic order</term>, and to all things of piety, and who is moved with a very particular inclination to favor this House, which for several reasons is not yet subject to

### **CHAPTER VI.**

*Of the spiritual love that one must have*
*for God, and for those who can*
*contribute to our salvation.*

**A**lthough I have made a long <note>The author refers to the preceding chapters where she diverged to discuss the practicalities of religious life and detachment.</note> digression: what I have said is so <margin>On the love of God, which is entirely spiritual.</margin> important that those who understand its consequence well will not, I am sure, blame me for it.
I return now to that love which we are not only permitted to have, but which it is useful for us to have. I say that it is purely spiritual; and in naming it so, I do not know if I know well what I am saying <note>A characteristic expression of humility; the author suggests that the experience of divine love is often beyond the reach of human language.</note>. It seems to me that it is not necessary to speak much of it, for fear that I have that few among you possess it; and if there is anyone whom Our Lord favors with such a grace, she <note>The author, St. Teresa of Avila, is writing specifically for her community of sisters (nuns), hence the feminine pronouns in the original French.</note> should praise Him greatly for it, because such a great gift will undoubtedly be accompanied by a very great perfection. I wish, nevertheless, to tell you...

<summary>This page marks a transition where the author returns from a thematic digression to define the "purely spiritual love" required for both God and those who assist in one's spiritual journey.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual love, salvation, divine grace, St. Teresa of Avila, monastic life, perfection</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the acquisition of virtue. The text is characteristic of 17th-century French spiritual literature, specifically reflecting the humble, self-deprecating tone of Saint Teresa of Avila in "The Way of Perfection."</meta>

58 ->THE WAY<-

...say something about it which might perhaps be useful; because those who desire to acquire virtue are drawn to it when it is laid out before their eyes. I confess that I do not know how I commit myself to speaking on this subject, believing as I do that I cannot clearly discern what is spiritual, nor when sensuality <note>In this context, "sensuality" refers to physical or worldly impulses that may be mistaken for or mingled with spiritual ones.</note> mingles with it. May God, if it please Him, make it known to me and make me capable of explaining it. I am like those people who hear talking from a distance without knowing what is being said: for sometimes I do not understand myself what I say, and yet God makes it so that it is well-spoken. At other times, what I say is irrelevant <note>original: "impertinent"; in this period, it meant nonsensical or poorly suited to the subject rather than rude.</note>; and that is what is most common for me.

It seems to me that when God clearly reveals to a person what this world is: that there is another world; the difference found between them; that one passes away like a dream, and that the other is eternal; what the Creator is, and what the creature <note>Technical term "creature" (French: creature) refers to any created being, particularly humans, as distinct from God the Creator.</note> is; what happiness it is to love the one, and what misfortune—

I pray to God with all my heart that this may be true, and that, knowing it as well as I hope, you may engrave it deeply upon your heart. ->66      THE WAY<-

...however many graces <note>In this context, "graces" refers to both physical beauty and charming qualities.</note> they are adorned with: although they please our eyes and give us cause to praise Him who, by creating them, made them so pleasant, those persons favored by God do not stop there. They do not allow themselves to reach the point of attaching their affection to these things, because it seems to them that this would be to love a thing of nothingness, and like embracing a shadow. This would cause them such great confusion <note>Here, "confusion" implies a sense of deep embarrassment or internal shame before God.</note> that they could not, without blushing with shame, say to God afterwards that they love Him.

<margin>To love only those who know how to contribute to our salvation.</margin>

You will perhaps tell me that these persons do not know what it is to love, or how to respond to the friendship shown to them. I answer that, at the very least, they care little about being loved themselves. And although at first their human nature may sometimes make them rejoice to see that they are loved, they no sooner look within themselves than they realize this is nothing but folly—except regarding those who can contribute to their salvation through their prayers or their teaching <note>original: "doctrine"</note>. All other affections weary them and [burden them]—

->ON PERFECTION. Ch. VI. 61<-

...annoy <note>The text begins mid-sentence, referring to how "perfect" souls react to human affections.</note> them, because they know that these affections can be of no benefit to them, and that they might even be capable of harming them. They do not fail to be grateful for it, and to repay this love by recommending to God those who love them.

For they consider the affection of these people as a debt for which Our Lord is responsible; because, seeing nothing in themselves that deserves to be loved, they believe that people only love them because God loves them. Thus, they leave to Him the care of repaying this love that others have for them; and by praying to Him with all their heart, they believe themselves released <note>original: "déchargées" — literally "unburdened" or "discharged" of a debt.</note> from this obligation, and remain as tranquil as if this affection did not affect them at all.

These considerations make me think sometimes that there is much blindness in this desire to be loved, unless it is—as I have said—by those who can help us to acquire eternal goods <term>eternal goods: spiritual virtues or rewards that lead to the soul's salvation in the afterlife.</term>. On which point it must be noted that while in worldly love we never love without some interest of utility or pleasure being involved; on the contrary, these person- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("personnes") and will continue on the next page.</note>

[souls] so perfect <note>The text begins mid-sentence, referring to "souls" from the previous page.</note> trample underfoot all the good that could be done for them, and all the satisfaction that could be given to them in the world; their soul being so disposed that, even if—so to speak—they desired it, they would be unable to find it anywhere but in God and in conversations where He alone is the entire subject. As they do not understand what advantage they could gain from being loved, they care little about being so; and they are so convinced of this truth that they laugh to themselves at the trouble they once took to know whether their affection was repaid with an equal affection. This is not to say that it is not very natural, even in **honest and permissible love** <note>original: "l’amour honneſte & permis"; this refers to virtuous, non-sinful human relationships such as family or friendship.</note>, to want to be loved when we love. If you then ask me: To what can these people then feel affection if they do not love what they see? I answer that they love what they see and feel affection for what they hear. But the things they see and they hear—

...unfortunate loves of the world, by which I do not mean those criminal and immodest loves, the mere name of which should fill us with horror. # ON PERFECTION. Ch. VII. 69

the slightest pain without us almost losing our patience; and so it goes for all the rest. For although our human weakness makes us feel the misfortunes of the person we love, our reason immediately comes to our aid. If we see that God grants this patience, the pain we felt is transformed into consolation and joy. This is true even though the affection we have for them makes us wish we could suffer in their place—if only we could, by suffering for them, acquire the same spiritual merit for them that is found in their own suffering. But all of this takes place without us feeling any inner disturbance or anxiety.

Why trouble yourself to serve God in a doubtful matter, since you can serve Him in so many others that are certain? ### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XVIII. 175

...obliges you to engage in this danger? I have expanded greatly upon this subject and judged it necessary, because I know the weakness of our nature. But God strengthens it when it pleases Him to elevate a soul to **contemplation** <term>contemplation: a high state of mystical prayer where the soul experiences a direct, quiet awareness of God, often distinguished from "meditation" which involves active thought</term>. As for those to whom He does not wish to grant this grace, I believed it my duty to give them these counsels, in which even the contemplatives themselves may find reason to humble themselves. I pray Our Lord to grant us, through His infinite goodness, the light that is necessary for us to accomplish His will in all things; and thus we shall have no cause to fear anything.

### CHAPTER XIX.

*On the Prayer that is made while meditating. On those whose minds wander during Prayer. Contemplation is like a source of living water. For nothing approaches the thirst they have to possess this thirst, because they know its price: and however great the pain it causes may be, it carries with it the remedy that makes it cease. So much so that it is a thirst which, by stifling the desire for the things of the earth, satisfies the soul in regard to those of Heaven. Among the properties of water, I remember that it has three which relate to my subject. The first is to refresh, for there is no heat so great that it does not dampen it: and it even extinguishes the greatest fires, unless they are artificial fires <note>The author refers to *feux d'artifice*. Its nature is to always kindle this divine fire more and more, and to spread it, if it were possible, throughout the whole world.

->### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XIX. 195<-

It may also be that the demon contributes greatly to increasing the intensity of this desire to die. It seems to me that Cassian <note>John Cassian (c. 360–435 AD) was a monk and theologian whose writings on the lives of the Desert Fathers were foundational to Western monasticism.</note> relates an example of this regarding a hermit whose life was very austere. This wretched spirit <note>A traditional reference to the Devil or a demonic influence.</note> persuaded the hermit to throw himself into a well, telling him that by doing so he would see God all the sooner. 

Regarding this, I believe that the life of this solitary man <term>Solitaire: a person, such as a hermit, who lives in total isolation to focus on spiritual devotion</term> had not been truly holy, nor his humility genuine. Otherwise, our Lord—being as good as He is and so faithful to His promises—would never have permitted him to be so blinded in a matter that is so clear. For it is evident that he would not have committed such a crime <note>In this historical context, suicide was viewed as a "crime" against both God and the self.</note> if this desire had come from God, who never inspires any impulses in us that are not accompanied by light <note>Divine clarity or spiritual understanding.</note>.

<summary>The author warns that an intense desire for death can be a demonic deception rather than a divine gift, citing Cassian's account of a hermit driven to suicide. The text argues that true divine inspiration always brings clarity and humility, preventing such "crimes."</summary>

Thus, they can be certain that they will never find themselves worse off for having begun to enter it, even if they leave it, because good never produces evil.

Thus, provided that one uses them only with this goal and intention, I do not disapprove of them; but otherwise, they would bring no benefit, and could be harmful without you even noticing.

They are undoubtedly right: and if our weakness were not so great, and our devotion so cold, we would have no need of other Prayers, nor of any Books to instruct us in prayer.

That is why, since I speak to persons who cannot find stillness <note>original: "se recueillir"; the act of inner recollection or withdrawing the senses to focus on God</note> by applying themselves to meditate on other Mysteries which seem to them too subtle and too refined, and since there are spirits so delicate that nothing is capable of satisfying them; I think it appropriate to establish here certain principles, certain means, and certain intentions of Prayer, without lingering on things that are too elevated. In this way, no one will be able to take your Books away from you, because provided that you set your heart on this and remain humble, you will have no need of anything more. ## CHAPTER XXII.

### *On Mental Prayer. That it must always be joined to vocal prayer. On the infinite perfections of God. Comparison of marriage with the union of the soul with God.*

<margin>On Mental
Prayer.</margin>

KNOW, my Daughters, that the difference in Prayer <term>Prayer: The original French "Oraison" refers to the act of communing with God, whether through set words or internal meditation.</term> should not be judged by our voice and our words, in such a way that when we speak it is "vocal," and when we are silent it is "mental." This refers to an internal, heart-to-heart conversation with God, distinct from merely reciting words.</note>. If you wish to also add vocal prayer, well and good; you may do so. # ON PERFECTION. Ch. XXIV. 233

I do not want even to mention in this place the names of **Mental Prayer** and **contemplation**, because I know for certain that there are several people whom these names alone frighten; it might also happen that someone of that sort would come to this House, because, as I have already said, not everyone walks along the same path.

What I wish to advise you now, then—and I can even say teach you, since it is permitted to me, my Daughters, as I hold the place of a mother to you through my office as Prioress <note>The superior or head of a convent of nuns.</note>—is the manner in which you must pray vocally. For it is right that you understand what you are saying. And because it may happen that those who cannot apply their minds to God also grow weary of long prayers, I will not speak of those. I will speak only of those prayers to which we are necessarily bound as Christians, which are the *Our Father* <note>original: "Pater noster"</note> and the *Hail Mary* <note>original: "Ave Maria"</note>, so that it cannot be said of us that we speak without knowing what—

->238 THE WAY<-

I agree that what I have just proposed can be called <term>mental prayer</term><note>Oraiſon mentale: A form of internal meditation where the person focuses on the meaning of the words and the presence of God rather than just reciting a formula.</note>. But I confess I do not understand how one can separate it from vocal prayer if one intends to perform it well, and to consider to whom one is speaking: for should we not strive to pay attention while praying? The best remedy that I find for this, after having tried it several times, is to strive to fix our mind on Him who prescribed this prayer to us. Do not, therefore, give way to impatience; but try to accustom yourselves to something that is so necessary for you.

<margin>That one can pass from vocal prayer to perfect contemplation.</margin>

NOW, so that you do not imagine, my daughters <note>The author, Saint Teresa of Avila, is writing this as a guide for her community of Carmelite nuns.</note>, that one draws little profit from vocal prayer <note>Prayers that are recited or spoken aloud, as opposed to silent meditation.</note> performed with the perfection I have described; I assure you that it may happen that while reciting the **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater"</note> or some other vocal prayer, God will cause us to pass all at once into a perfect contemplation <term>contemplation: a state of "infused" prayer where the soul feels a direct, passive connection to the divine, rather than actively thinking or imagining</term>. It is in this way that He makes us know that He listens to the person who speaks to Him, and He humbles His greatness so far as to deign to speak to them also, by holding their spirit as if in suspense, stopping their thoughts, and binding their tongue in such a way that, even if they wished to, they could not utter a

<margin>On perfect contemplation.</margin>

Those people whom Our Lord favors with such grace find themselves enjoying this happiness without knowing how they enjoy it. They find themselves set on fire with love without knowing how they love. And they find that they possess what they love without knowing how they possess it. All they can do is realize that the understanding could not go so far as to imagine, nor could desire go so far as to wish for, a good as great as that which they enjoy. Their will embraces it without knowing in what manner it does so; and according to the little that these souls are capable of understanding, they see that this good is of such great value that all the labors of the earth

The first ones he utters will make you know the extreme love he bears for you. And what can be sweeter or more agreeable to a good disciple than to see that his Master loves him?

->CHAPTER XXVII.<-

This is the greatest good a holy soul can desire in the world, and I cannot express it enough with my words, since this protection from God is the greatest assurance we can have on earth.

Our Lord, having seen how important it is to awaken these souls to remind them that they have enemies who force them to always stand on their guard; and that the higher they are elevated, the more they need the help of His eternal Father; since in falling, they would fall from a greater height: And wanting, moreover, to deliver them from the snares where they...

Work continually, my **Sisters**, <margin>On humility.</margin> to acquire humility: Recognize that you are not worthy of these favors, and do not seek them out. By this means, the devil, instead of winning souls, loses many (in my opinion) of those whose ruin he thinks he can bring about; and God draws our good from the evil that the enemy intended to do us. For the Lord is faithful in His promises, and seeing that our intention in **Prayer** is to please Him and to serve Him, He remains...

### ON PERFECTION. Ch. XXXVIII. 383

...[con]fidence, <note>The text begins mid-word with "-furance," likely "assurance," meaning a false sense of security or overconfidence.</note> one falls without noticing into a trap from which one cannot escape. For even though it is not a visible mortal sin <term>mortal sin: in Catholic theology, a grave sin that destroys the state of grace</term> capable of plunging the soul into hell, it weakens it in such a way that it can no longer walk in this path of which I have begun to speak to you.

I assure you that this temptation is very perilous; and I have so much experience with it that I can speak to you boldly about it, though not as well as I would wish. What remedy then is there, my Sisters? I find no better one than that which our divine Master <note>Referring to Jesus Christ</note> teaches us, which is to pray in this Prayer <note>The Lord's Prayer, specifically the petition "lead us not into temptation"</note> to his eternal Father that He not allow us to succumb to temptation. I will add another remedy: if it seems to us that Our Lord has given us some virtue, we must consider it as a gift <note>original: "un bien," literally a good or an asset</note> we have received from Him, which He can take away from us at any hour, as often happens by the order of Providence. Have you never experienced this, my Daughters?

<summary>This page warns against the subtle danger of spiritual overconfidence, which weakens the soul's progress even if it doesn't lead to grave sin. The author recommends humility and the Lord's Prayer as remedies, reminding her readers that all virtues are temporary gifts from God.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual temptation, mortal sin, humility, Lord's Prayer, divine grace, Providence, Saint Teresa of Avila</keywords>

### OF PERFECTION Ch. XXXVIII. 385

I would like to be out of the world, <note>In monastic writings, "the world" refers to secular life and its distractions, which are seen as obstacles to spiritual focus.</note> so much does everything I see in it displease me. In all this I am not alone: for I have noticed the same things in several people better than I, and I know that in fact they happen in this way.

Now if this is so, my Sisters, who will be the one who can say that their soul is enriched with virtues, since at the time when one has the most need of them, one finds that one has none? Let us then take great care not to conceive such thoughts <note>The "thoughts" of pride or self-assurance regarding one's own holiness.</note>. Let us recognize on the contrary that we are poor, and let us not run into debt without having the means to pay, by attributing to ourselves virtues that do not belong to us. The treasure of our soul is in the hands of God and not in our own; and we do not know when it will please him to leave us in the prison of our poverty and our misery without giving us anything. How do we know if, when others hold us to be good and we believe ourselves to be so, he will continue to share his <term>graces</term> <note>In Christian theology, "grace" refers to the free and unmerited favor of God, which provides the spiritual strength to live a virtuous life.</note> with us: or if he will not wish to—

# 402 THE WAY

## CHAPTER XL.

**That the love and fear of God joined together make a powerful remedy for resisting the temptations of the demon. What shall be, at death, the misfortune of those who have not loved God, and the happiness of those who have loved him.**

<margin>Resisting the temptations of the demon by love and by the fear of God.</margin>

O my dear Master, give us some means of protecting ourselves from the snares of our enemies in such a perilous war. The means that His Divine Majesty <note>A common title used by St. Teresa of Avila to refer to God, reflecting a relationship of both deep intimacy and profound royal respect.</note> gives us, my daughters, and which we can use boldly, is to always maintain both love and fear. Love will urge us to move forward, and fear will make us take care where we step, so that we do not fall on a path where so many things can make us stumble—as happens on almost every path one walks in this life. This shall be the true way to avoid being deceived.

<summary>This page explains how the dual virtues of love and fear act as a safeguard for the soul, providing both the motivation to progress and the caution necessary to avoid spiritual pitfalls.</summary>

<keywords>love of God, fear of God, temptation, spiritual warfare, St. Teresa of Avila, divine majesty, spiritual protection</keywords>

You will perhaps ask me how you might know that you possess these great virtues, and you are right to ask, since it is certain that you could never be entirely sure of it. For if you were certain of having a true love of God, you would also be certain of being in <term>grace<note>In the 17th-century context, "being in grace" means being in a state of friendship with God, free from mortal sin. Catholic doctrine taught that one could not have absolute "certainty of faith" regarding their own salvation or state of grace.</note></term>. There are nonetheless, my daughters, such evident signs of this, that it seems even the blind could see them. They are neither secret nor hidden; but they make so much noise, that even if you did not wish to, you could not fail to hear them.

The number of those who possess these two qualities in perfection is so small that they are easily noticed by their rarity; they are known all the more because the more they remain in silence and in secrecy, the more they stand out. This love and this fear of God are like two strongholds from which one wages war against the world and the <term>demon<note>A common term in spiritual manuals of this era referring to the Devil or the personification of evil and temptation.</note></term>. Those who truly love God love everything that is good, desire everything that is good, favor everything that is good, praise everything that is good, and join themselves—

<margin>Concerning the fear of God.</margin> I come now to what concerns the fear of God <note>In the spiritual tradition of this period, "fear of God" does not mean a terrifying dread of punishment, but rather a "filial fear"—a deep, loving reverence and a desire not to offend a beloved Father.</note>, although I feel some regret in not saying something about that love of the world with which I am so well acquainted, and which I would like you to understand so that you might be entirely delivered from it. Whoever possesses the fear of God perceives it easily, and those who interact with him have no trouble noticing it. <meta>This page continues Chapter 41 of "The Way of Perfection" (De la Perfection). The text focuses on the spiritual dangers of rigidity and anxiety within a religious community.</meta>

->OF PERFECTION Ch. XLI. 421<-

Lord. I do not know any other reason for it; but it usually happens.

I urge you, my Daughters, to flee from anxiety and constraint <note>original: "la gêne & la contrainte"; in this context, it refers to a state of spiritual stiffness or being "tied up" in one's own rules</note>, because the soul that gives in to them finds itself thereby ill-disposed to every kind of good, and sometimes falls into scrupules <term>scrupules: a religious term for obsessive anxiety or guilt over minor or imagined sins, which can paralyze a person's spiritual life</term> which make it useless to itself and to others.

For if, remaining constrained in this way, the soul does not fall into these scrupules; even though she may be good for herself, she will be incapable of serving others to help them advance in piety, because this constraint is so much an enemy of our nature that it intimidates and frightens us. Thus, even though these people may be convinced that the path you follow is better than the one they walk, the fear of falling into these anxieties and constraints where they see you will make them lose the desire they had to enter upon it.

This constraint you might feel would also produce another evil; which is that seeing others walk by a different path, by dealing freely <note>The text cuts off here, but "traiting librement" suggests a contrast between the rigid soul and those who interact with others or God with spiritual liberty</note>...

<summary>In this passage, the author warns her nuns against spiritual rigidity and obsessive anxiety, arguing that such behavior makes them useless to others and discourages newcomers from joining their path.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual liberty, scrupules, religious life, piety, St. Teresa of Avila, anxiety, monastic discipline</keywords>

422
->THE WAY<-

...with one's neighbor <note>The term "neighbor" (prochain) here refers to anyone outside oneself, particularly those the nuns might interact with for spiritual benefit.</note> to contribute to their salvation: although this way of acting might be more perfect, you might imagine there is imperfection in it, and condemn as a defect or an excess the completely holy joy that these people might show in these encounters. This is perilous, especially for us who have no formal knowledge <note>original: "science"; in this 17th-century context, this refers to specialized theological study or the "science of the saints."</note>, and who consequently do not know how to discern what can be done without sin. 

Besides, this is to be in a continual and very dangerous temptation, because it works to the prejudice of the neighbor. And it is also very wrong to imagine that all those who do not walk as you do in this "path of constraint" <note>This refers to a rigid, highly restricted way of living or practicing faith.</note> are not on the right way. To which one can add another inconvenience, which is that on certain occasions where your duty would oblige you to speak, this scrupulous <term>scrupulous: a state of spiritual anxiety where a person excessively fears they are committing a sin in every action, leading to indecision or harsh judgment.</term> fear of exceeding in something would hold you back, or would perhaps make you speak well of that for which you should show horror.

Try then, my daughters, as much...

<meta>This page continues an exhortation on how a person seeking spiritual perfection should interact with others, emphasizing that holiness should be expressed through kindness and social grace rather than through a stern or off-putting demeanor.</meta>

->**ON PERFECTION. Ch. XLI. 423**<-

...as much as you can without offending God, to conduct yourself in such a way toward all those with whom you must live that they remain satisfied with your conversation; that they desire to be able to imitate your way of acting, and that virtue appears so beautiful and so lovable in your interactions, that instead of making them afraid, it inspires in them respect and love.

   This advice is very important to Religious women <note>The term "Religieuses" refers to nuns or women living in a formal religious community under vows.</note>. The holier they are, the more they must strive to show sweetness and kindness toward their Sisters. ### CHAPTER XLII.

*On the final words of the Lord's Prayer:* <note>original: "Pater"</note>
*But deliver us from evil.*

426 [space] THE WAY

It is true, I admit, that we do not pass our life in such great poverty, such great labors, and such great sufferings as our divine Redeemer passed his own. For what was his whole life but a continual death, since the cruel torment <note>The original French uses "supplice," which refers to an agonizing physical punishment or execution.</note> that the Jews were to make him suffer, and which he had always before his eyes, was the least of his torments? His great sorrow was to see his Father offended in so many ways, and so many souls unhappily lost. 

If this would be a very great cause of affliction to a person who possessed charity: in what way was the boundless charity of our Lord not touched by it? Thus, did he not have great reason to pray to his Father to deliver him from so many pains, to let him enjoy an eternal rest in his Kingdom, of which he was the true heir? This is why he adds these words: *So be it.* <note>original: "Ainsi soit-il." This is the traditional French translation of the Hebrew term "Amen," used to conclude prayers.</note>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Chapter 42 of "The Way of Perfection," where the author reflects on her commentary on the Lord's Prayer.</meta>

# ON PERFECTION Ch. XLII. 431

It is true that after having finished speaking of this prayer <note>Teresa refers here to the Lord's Prayer (the Pater Noster), which she has been analyzing line-by-line in the preceding chapters.</note>, I could not go any further. And I think, my Sisters, that God wanted thereby to make us understand how great the consolation is that it contains. It is such that even people who do not know how to read could, if they understood it well, find so much benefit in it that they would draw from it both much instruction and a great relief in their troubles.

   Let us learn then, my Daughters, to humble ourselves by considering with what humility our good Master teaches us; and pray Him to forgive me for the boldness I have taken in speaking of such sublime things <note>original: "choſes ſi relevées" — referring to the deep mystical and theological interpretations of the prayer.</note>, since obedience alone made me do it. His Divine Majesty knows that I was incapable of it, if He had not taught me what I had to say to you. Thank Him, my Sisters, for this grace which He has undoubtedly granted me only in consideration of the humility with which you desired this of me, and wished to be instructed by a person as wretched as I am.

If Father Dominic, a *Presentado* <note>original: "Pere Preſenté Dominique" — This refers to Fray Domingo Bañez, a famous Dominican theologian and Teresa's confessor. "Presentado" was an academic title within the Dominican Order for those eligible for a mastership in theology.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa concludes her commentary on the Lord's Prayer, expressing her own inadequacy and crediting God's grace and the sisters' humility for the insights shared.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Teresa of Avila, Lord's Prayer, humility, obedience, Domingo Bañez, Dominican Order, prayer for the illiterate, spiritual consolation</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page concludes the text of "The Way of Perfection" by St. Teresa of Avila. It follows the final spiritual instructions of the preceding chapters, serving as a formal closing and submission to church authority.</meta>

432 THE WAY OF PERFECTION. Ch. XLII.

If my Confessor <note>Teresa’s spiritual director, whose role was to review her writings for theological correctness before they were shared.</note>, to whom I shall give this writing before you see it, judges that it may be useful to you and places it in your hands, I will have no small consolation from the comfort you receive from it. But if he finds that it is not worthy of being seen, you will be satisfied, if you please, with my goodwill, since I have obeyed what you commanded me; and I will consider myself very well rewarded for the trouble I took in writing it. I say *writing* it, having certainly had no trouble thinking of what I should say <note>Teresa often claimed her words were inspired by God or flowed without intellectual strain.</note>. Let us bless and praise our Lord forever, from whom alone proceeds all the good that we think, that we say, and that we do. Amen <note>original: "Ainsi soit-il," literally "So be it."</note>.

For it is to place our soul in peace and in calm, or to put it better, it is to feel that Our Lord places it there through His divine presence, establishing all its <term>faculties and powers</term> <note>In 17th-century spirituality, the "faculties" usually refer to the memory, understanding, and will of the soul.</note> in a state of complete rest, just as we see in the Gospel how He acted in the same way toward Simeon the Just.

It sees only that it finds itself in this Kingdom: that it is close to its King; and that He has resolved to give it to her: but her reverence is so great that she does not dare to ask it of Him.

   It is like an interior and exterior fainting <term>évanouïſſement: in this context, a spiritual swoon or "quieting" of the senses where the soul is overwhelmed by divine presence</term> all at once, during which the body wishes to remain without moving, just like the traveler who, having almost arrived where he wishes to go, rests so that he may arrive even sooner through the doubling of strength that his forces receive from this rest. But if the body finds itself overwhelmed with pleasure, that which the soul enjoys is no less. Its joy at seeing itself near this heavenly fountain is so great that, even before drinking from it, it finds itself satisfied. It seems to the soul that it has nothing more to desire: all its powers <term>puiſſances: the "faculties" of the soul—the memory, understanding, and will—which in this state of prayer are stilled or "satisfied" by God</term> are so satisfied that they

St. Teresa uses the metaphors of a resting traveler and a heavenly fountain to illustrate this peaceful transition toward divine union.</summary>

...often joins such a favor to this other one. This is what it consists of: when this <term>Quietude</term> <note>A state of deep interior peace and silent prayer where the soul feels the presence of God.</note> is great and lasts for a long time, it seems to me that if the **will** <note>In the psychology of the time, the "will" was the faculty of the soul responsible for loving and choosing.</note> were not attached and, as it were, bound, it could not preserve the peace it enjoys—just as it does when one remains in this state for a day or two, without understanding how it happens. 

These persons see clearly that they are not entirely occupied by what they are doing; rather, the most important part of them is elsewhere—that is, the **will**—which in my opinion is then united to God. This leaves the other <term>faculties</term> <note>Referring to the memory and the intellect/understanding.</note> free to occupy themselves with things regarding His service, for which they are much better suited than at any other time. 

But as for the things of the world, they are so incapable of them that they seem as if numb, and sometimes entirely bewildered. This is a great favor that God grants to those whom He chooses to grant it, because the **active and contemplative life** <note>The "active life" refers to outward work and charity (like Martha), while the "contemplative life" refers to inner prayer and devotion (like Mary).</note> are found joined together; and during this happy time, Our Lord...

This gives me reason to believe that since the soul is so fully satisfied in this **Prayer of Quiet** <term>Prayer of Quiet: a stage of mystical prayer where the soul experiences a deep, peaceful sense of God’s presence, often while the other faculties of the mind remain active.</term>, it is very likely that most often the will is united with Him who alone is capable of filling it with happiness. And because there are some among you whom Our Lord, through His goodness alone, has favored with this grace, it seems to me that it would not be out of place for me to give them some advice on this subject.

   Therefore, when the will finds itself in this tranquility and **quietude**, it should no longer take any more account of the understanding, or the thought, or the imagination—for I do not know which of these three names is the most fitting—than it would of a fool or a madman. It should not occupy itself by trying to drag these faculties along by force, for fear of turning away and becoming unsettled. From such an effort, not only would the soul fail to gain any greater profit from its **Prayer** <term>Oraison: a specific type of contemplative, interior prayer focused on the heart rather than spoken words</term> by this means, but all its efforts would only serve to make it lose what God had given it without its having contributed anything at all.

   Here is a comparison that Our Lord put into my mind one day during Prayer, which in my opinion explains this quite clearly; therefore, I ask you to consider it well. The soul in this state resembles an infant who is still nursing; whose mother, to caress the child while it is in her arms, lets the milk flow into its mouth without the child even having to move its lips.

<margin>
Difference between the **Prayer of Quiet** and that of **union**.
